I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work, at home, or in both settings?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m trying to get work done—well, not a job exactly, but like school or project-type stuff at home. If I’m working on a dashboard or something, one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or a random idea for another project can pull me off track, and then I realize I’ve totally lost what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as both settings, but it’s mainly whenever I’m supposed to be focusing on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get pulled away more easily when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think maybe it’s a little worse when I’m overwhelmed, but honestly it happens even when I’m not super stressed. Like, the main thing is just how easily I get pulled off track—one Slack ping, or a song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of another project, and then I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of the reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think so. Not in a huge way, I don’t think, but in meetings or portfolio reviews I’ll realize I’m bouncing my leg or clicking my pen, or twisting my rings. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair without really noticing until someone looks at me, and then I try to stop. Does that count as restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I try not to, because I know it’s rude, but when people are brainstorming I can get a little too excited and jump in before they’re fully done explaining. Like I’ll blurt out an idea, or even start adding something to their Figma board before they’ve finished walking through it. Then afterward I’m like, “Oh, that probably came across kind of pushy,” even though I didn’t mean it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really, no. I mean, it’s maybe gotten easier to hide as I’ve gotten older, but looking back it was there pretty early.

I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work, at home, or in both settings?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m trying to get work done—well, not a job exactly, but like school or project-type stuff at home. If I’m working on a dashboard or something, one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or a random idea for another project can pull me off track, and then I realize I’ve totally lost what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as both settings, but it’s mainly whenever I’m supposed to be focusing on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get pulled away more easily when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think maybe it’s a little worse when I’m overwhelmed, but honestly it happens even when I’m not super stressed. Like, the main thing is just how easily I get pulled off track—one Slack ping, or a song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of another project, and then I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of the reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think so. Not in a huge way, I don’t think, but in meetings or portfolio reviews I’ll realize I’m bouncing my leg or clicking my pen, or twisting my rings. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair without really noticing until someone looks at me, and then I try to stop. Does that count as restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I try not to, because I know it’s rude, but when people are brainstorming I can get a little too excited and jump in before they’re fully done explaining. Like I’ll blurt out an idea, or even start adding something to their Figma board before they’ve finished walking through it. Then afterward I’m like, “Oh, that probably came across kind of pushy,” even though I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in line or when waiting to contribute to a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I mean, I can wait, it’s not like I’m trying to cut people off, but during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time or shifting around, and if I have something to say I have this urge to jump in before it’s actually my turn. I usually try to hold it in, but it’s definitely uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, but it does happen. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining an idea, I’ll sometimes blurt out what I’m thinking before they’ve finished. Or I’ll start adding something to the Figma board while they’re still talking, because I’m excited and I think I know where they’re going. Then afterward I realize it probably came off kind of pushy, which I don’t want. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and impulsivity causing significant problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it has, though I always feel bad saying it that dramatically. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I have lost a couple small freelance contracts because I didn’t follow through as cleanly or reliably as people needed. And I missed out on a portfolio competition for similar reasons.\n\nAlso, some friends and collaborators still like my ideas, I think, but they’re more hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be consistent. That’s probably the part that bothers me most. Does that count as significant?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't have these problems with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s maybe gotten easier to hide as I’ve gotten older, but looking back it was there pretty early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like that I rushed through worksheets, or I lost track of directions, or talked over other kids during group work. And my parents would sit next to me during homework because I’d kind of wander off or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but… yeah, I don’t think there was a whole period where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s not, like, completely out of control or anything, but I get pulled away really easily—like if there’s a Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or I suddenly think of another project, then I realize I’ve lost the thread of what I was supposed to be finishing, like a dashboard. I wasn’t sure if that’s the kind of thing to come in for, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Does this happen to you at work, at home, or in both settings?\n\nPatient: Mostly when I’m trying to get work done—well, not a job exactly, but like school or project-type stuff at home. If I’m working on a dashboard or something, one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or a random idea for another project can pull me off track, and then I realize I’ve totally lost what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as both settings, but it’s mainly whenever I’m supposed to be focusing on something.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get pulled away more easily when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think maybe it’s a little worse when I’m overwhelmed, but honestly it happens even when I’m not super stressed. Like, the main thing is just how easily I get pulled off track—one Slack ping, or a song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I suddenly think of another project, and then I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic, but that’s kind of the reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think so. Not in a huge way, I don’t think, but in meetings or portfolio reviews I’ll realize I’m bouncing my leg or clicking my pen, or twisting my rings. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair without really noticing until someone looks at me, and then I try to stop. Does that count as restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or finish their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I try not to, because I know it’s rude, but when people are brainstorming I can get a little too excited and jump in before they’re fully done explaining. Like I’ll blurt out an idea, or even start adding something to their Figma board before they’ve finished walking through it. Then afterward I’m like, “Oh, that probably came across kind of pushy,” even though I didn’t mean it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in line or when waiting to contribute to a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I mean, I can wait, it’s not like I’m trying to cut people off, but during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time or shifting around, and if I have something to say I have this urge to jump in before it’s actually my turn. I usually try to hold it in, but it’s definitely uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, but it does happen. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining an idea, I’ll sometimes blurt out what I’m thinking before they’ve finished. Or I’ll start adding something to the Figma board while they’re still talking, because I’m excited and I think I know where they’re going. Then afterward I realize it probably came off kind of pushy, which I don’t want. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and impulsivity causing significant problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it has, though I always feel bad saying it that dramatically. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I have lost a couple small freelance contracts because I didn’t follow through as cleanly or reliably as people needed. And I missed out on a portfolio competition for similar reasons.\n\nAlso, some friends and collaborators still like my ideas, I think, but they’re more hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be consistent. That’s probably the part that bothers me most. Does that count as significant?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't have these problems with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s maybe gotten easier to hide as I’ve gotten older, but looking back it was there pretty early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like that I rushed through worksheets, or I lost track of directions, or talked over other kids during group work. And my parents would sit next to me during homework because I’d kind of wander off or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but… yeah, I don’t think there was a whole period where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. Not constantly or anything, but enough that I remember it.\n\nIn elementary school, teachers would write comments like I rushed through worksheets, or I’d lose track of the directions, or I’d talk over other kids during group work. And at home my parents would sometimes sit beside me while I did homework because otherwise I’d kind of drift off or start something else before finishing.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of comment you mean, but… yeah, it was noticed.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it was pretty consistent.\n\nLike even in elementary school, teachers would write those kinds of comments — that I rushed through worksheets, or lost track of directions, or talked over classmates during group work. And my parents used to sit beside me when I did homework because if they didn’t, I’d kind of wander off or start something else before finishing.\n\nSo… yeah, it wasn’t just one bad year or anything. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your romantic relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way, I guess, but it has caused some tension.\n\nMore with friends or people I’m collaborating with than, like, romance specifically. I’ve had situations where people liked my ideas, but then I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I missed something, and they became kind of hesitant to work with me again. I’ve even lost a couple small freelance-type opportunities, and I missed out on a portfolio competition because I wasn’t reliable enough with the follow-through.\n\nI don’t think people hate me for it or anything, but… yeah, I can tell it affects how much they trust me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on track and getting pulled away easily are more of a problem for you than the restlessness and impulsivity you've described?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the restlessness and blurting things out can be annoying, but the getting pulled off track is honestly the main reason I came in.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be trying to finish a dashboard or something, and one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project pops up, and suddenly I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it really does derail me.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that part is more of the problem for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and getting pulled away easily are related to how interesting or boring the task is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can be, but it’s not only boring tasks, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, the dashboard example — I do care about it, and it’s not necessarily boring to me. But one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project can pull me away, and then I suddenly realize I’ve lost the thread of what I was supposed to be finishing.\n\nSo interest helps a little, maybe, but it doesn’t fully protect me from getting sidetracked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with focus and getting pulled away if you were doing something you really enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would, honestly. Maybe a little less if I’m really into it, but it doesn’t totally go away.\n\nLike with the dashboard stuff, I do actually care about it, and I can be interested in it, but then one Slack ping happens, or some song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I get a random idea for another project, and I’m suddenly not where I meant to be mentally. Then I’m like, wait, what was I trying to finish?\n\nSo… yes, even with things I enjoy, I can still get pulled off track pretty easily. That’s kind of the main thing I’m worried about, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project can pull me away, and then I suddenly realize I’ve lost the thread of what I was supposed to be finishing.\n\nSo interest helps a little, maybe, but it doesn’t fully protect me from getting sidetracked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with focus and getting pulled away if you were doing something you really enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would, honestly. Maybe a little less if I’m really into it, but it doesn’t totally go away.\n\nLike with the dashboard stuff, I do actually care about it, and I can be interested in it, but then one Slack ping happens, or some song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I get a random idea for another project, and I’m suddenly not where I meant to be mentally. Then I’m like, wait, what was I trying to finish?\n\nSo… yes, even with things I enjoy, I can still get pulled off track pretty easily. That’s kind of the main thing I’m worried about, I guess.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find it difficult to focus and stay on track in situations where you're doing something you really enjoy, like a hobby?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"The main reason I came in is that I get pulled off track so easily—one Slack ping, a song lyric, or a random idea for another project, and suddenly I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so… even with things I like, unfortunately.

It’s not like I’m totally unable to focus all the time, but I do get pulled off track really easily. Like I was saying, one Slack ping, or even just a song lyric in my head or some random idea for another project, and suddenly I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing.

So I guess the answer is yes — it still happens even when I’m interested. I’m not sure if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but that’s what it feels like.

And my parents would sit next to me during homework because I’d kind of wander off or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but… yeah, I don’t think there was a whole period where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. Not constantly or anything, but enough that I remember it.\n\nIn elementary school, teachers would write comments like I rushed through worksheets, or I’d lose track of the directions, or I’d talk over other kids during group work. I’ve even lost a couple small freelance-type opportunities, and I missed out on a portfolio competition because I wasn’t reliable enough with the follow-through.\n\nI don’t think people hate me for it or anything, but… yeah, I can tell it affects how much they trust me. Not constantly or anything, but enough that I remember it.\n\nIn elementary school, teachers would write comments like I rushed through worksheets, or I’d lose track of the directions, or I’d talk over other kids during group work. And at home my parents would sometimes sit beside me while I did homework because otherwise I’d kind of drift off or start something else before finishing.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of comment you mean, but… yeah, it was noticed.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it was pretty consistent.\n\nLike even in elementary school, teachers would write those kinds of comments — that I rushed through worksheets, or lost track of directions, or talked over classmates during group work. And my parents used to sit beside me when I did homework because if they didn’t, I’d kind of wander off or start something else before finishing.\n\nSo… yeah, it wasn’t just one bad year or anything. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your romantic relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way, I guess, but it has caused some tension.\n\nMore with friends or people I’m collaborating with than, like, romance specifically. I’ve had situations where people liked my ideas, but then I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I missed something, and they became kind of hesitant to work with me again. I’ve even lost a couple small freelance-type opportunities, and I missed out on a portfolio competition because I wasn’t reliable enough with the follow-through.\n\nI don’t think people hate me for it or anything, but… yeah, I can tell it affects how much they trust me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on track and getting pulled away easily are more of a problem for you than the restlessness and impulsivity you've described?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. But one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project can pull me away, and then I suddenly realize I’ve lost the thread of what I was supposed to be finishing.\n\nSo interest helps a little, maybe, but it doesn’t fully protect me from getting sidetracked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with focus and getting pulled away if you were doing something you really enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would, honestly. Maybe a little less if I’m really into it, but it doesn’t totally go away.\n\nLike with the dashboard stuff, I do actually care about it, and I can be interested in it, but then one Slack ping happens, or some song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I get a random idea for another project, and I’m suddenly not where I meant to be mentally. I mean, I can wait, it’s not like I’m trying to cut people off, but during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues I get really antsy. I’ll keep checking the time or shifting around, and if I have something to say I have this urge to jump in before it’s actually my turn. I usually try to hold it in, but it’s definitely uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before questions have been completed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, but it does happen. Especially when people are brainstorming or explaining an idea, I’ll sometimes blurt out what I’m thinking before they’ve finished. Or I’ll start adding something to the Figma board while they’re still talking, because I’m excited and I think I know where they’re going. Then afterward I realize it probably came off kind of pushy, which I don’t want. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with focus and impulsivity causing significant problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I think it has, though I always feel bad saying it that dramatically. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but I have lost a couple small freelance contracts because I didn’t follow through as cleanly or reliably as people needed. And I missed out on a portfolio competition for similar reasons.\n\nAlso, some friends and collaborators still like my ideas, I think, but they’re more hesitant to work with me because they’re not sure I’ll be consistent. That’s probably the part that bothers me most. Does that count as significant?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you didn't have these problems with focus and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s maybe gotten easier to hide as I’ve gotten older, but looking back it was there pretty early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things like that I rushed through worksheets, or I lost track of directions, or talked over other kids during group work. And my parents would sit next to me during homework because I’d kind of wander off or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I was a terrible student or anything, but… yeah, I don’t think there was a whole period where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did. Not constantly or anything, but enough that I remember it.\n\nIn elementary school, teachers would write comments like I rushed through worksheets, or I’d lose track of the directions, or I’d talk over other kids during group work. And at home my parents would sometimes sit beside me while I did homework because otherwise I’d kind of drift off or start something else before finishing.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of comment you mean, but… yeah, it was noticed.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but it was pretty consistent.\n\nLike even in elementary school, teachers would write those kinds of comments — that I rushed through worksheets, or lost track of directions, or talked over classmates during group work. And my parents used to sit beside me when I did homework because if they didn’t, I’d kind of wander off or start something else before finishing.\n\nSo… yeah, it wasn’t just one bad year or anything. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your romantic relationships or friendships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a huge dramatic way, I guess, but it has caused some tension.\n\nMore with friends or people I’m collaborating with than, like, romance specifically. I’ve had situations where people liked my ideas, but then I didn’t follow through cleanly enough, or I missed something, and they became kind of hesitant to work with me again. I’ve even lost a couple small freelance-type opportunities, and I missed out on a portfolio competition because I wasn’t reliable enough with the follow-through.\n\nI don’t think people hate me for it or anything, but… yeah, I can tell it affects how much they trust me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with staying on track and getting pulled away easily are more of a problem for you than the restlessness and impulsivity you've described?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, the restlessness and blurting things out can be annoying, but the getting pulled off track is honestly the main reason I came in.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be trying to finish a dashboard or something, and one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project pops up, and suddenly I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was doing. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it really does derail me.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that part is more of the problem for me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and getting pulled away easily are related to how interesting or boring the task is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it can be, but it’s not only boring tasks, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, the dashboard example — I do care about it, and it’s not necessarily boring to me. But one Slack ping, or even a song lyric, or some random idea for another project can pull me away, and then I suddenly realize I’ve lost the thread of what I was supposed to be finishing.\n\nSo interest helps a little, maybe, but it doesn’t fully protect me from getting sidetracked. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have these problems with focus and getting pulled away if you were doing something you really enjoyed?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I still would, honestly. Maybe a little less if I’m really into it, but it doesn’t totally go away.\n\nLike with the dashboard stuff, I do actually care about it, and I can be interested in it, but then one Slack ping happens, or some song lyric gets stuck in my head, or I get a random idea for another project, and I’m suddenly not where I meant to be mentally. That’s kind of the main thing I’m worried about, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus and stay on track in situations where you're doing something you really enjoy, like a hobby?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… even with things I like, unfortunately.\n\nIt’s not like I’m totally unable to focus all the time, but I do get pulled off track really easily. Like I was saying, one Slack ping, or even just a song lyric in my head or some random idea for another project, and suddenly I’ve lost the thread of the dashboard I was supposed to be finishing.\n\nSo I guess the answer is yes — it still happens even when I’m interested. I’m not sure if that’s the “right” way to describe it, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or ADHD by a healthcare professional?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not formally, anyway.\n\nI’ve wondered about it, obviously, since I’m here, but I haven’t had a healthcare professional actually diagnose me with it before. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus and impulsivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, because I did okay in school overall, but even in elementary school there were comments from teachers about me rushing through worksheets or losing track of the directions.\n\nAnd during group work I guess I would talk over other kids sometimes. My parents also used to sit next to me while I did homework because if they didn’t, I’d sort of wander off or start doing something else before I finished.\n\nDoes that count as the kind of thing you mean before 12?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and impulsivity have been present for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. More than six months, definitely.\n\nI’d say it’s been causing actual problems for about a year and five months now — since I moved out of more structured school projects and started trying to build my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio. I don’t want to overstate it, because I can still get things done sometimes, but that’s when it became noticeably harder to manage on my own.\n\nIs that the kind of time frame you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it shows up in more than one area, not just one situation. I mean, I try to keep it manageable, and I don’t think people see me as totally unreliable or anything, but… it has affected school-related portfolio stuff and freelance-type projects.

I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts, and I missed out on a portfolio competition because I didn’t follow through cleanly enough. And with friends or collaborators, it’s a little embarrassing, but some people seem hesitant to work with me now, even though they like my ideas.

So… yes, I guess it has been consistent across settings, if that’s what you’re asking?
